For the past year or so I’m always puttering around—fixing a drawer, arranging things, signing up to help with one more little volunteer thing. My daughter says, “Mother, just sit down,” but then I notice a crooked picture frame or some papers that need sorting, and off I go. Even at 87, I feel like I ought to be able to rest, but being still is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble relaxing, even when you’re not busy with tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not constant, exactly, but if I’m sitting through the announcements at church, or one of those senior center meetings where they read every little item—well, I get this ants-in-my-chair feeling. I’ll shift around and fuss with my papers, or I’ll decide I need coffee just so I can stand up for a minute. It’s silly, because I’m not in a hurry to be anywhere, but my body acts like I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things, even when there’s no clear reason to be?\n\nPatient: Hmm… worried? I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, at my age there are always little things to think about—appointments, whether I left the kettle on, and my neighbor’s cat keeps wandering into my garden, which is another story—but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around feeling anxious for no reason. At least, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy, even if you’re not doing much?\n\nPatient: Tired? Well… I don’t know. At 87, everyone expects you to say yes, don’t they? I do sit down sometimes and think, “Goodness, that was enough for now,” but then I remember the laundry in the dryer or that I meant to call about the church flowers—last year they had the lilies delivered too early and the whole hallway smelled like a florist shop.\n\nSo, lacking energy… I’m not sure I’d say that. I haven’t really noticed being unusually tired when I’m not doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities you used to like—like seeing friends, gardening, or going to church events?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, really. I still like being around people when I go, and the garden is… well, the garden is more work than it used to be, but that may be because the weeds seem to have gotten more ambitious than I have.\n\nChurch events can be lovely, though sometimes they do go on, especially when everyone needs to make an announcement. But not enjoying things—hmm. I don’t think I’d say that. I’d have to think about it more, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing or that you can’t slow your mind down, even when you want to relax?\n\nPatient: Racing thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d call it that. Sometimes when I’m sitting down, I’ll think of one thing and then another, like whether I put the bins out or if I should write a note to Margaret—oh, she never answers her phone anyway, always says she can’t hear it ringing—but I don’t know that my mind feels like it’s racing exactly.\n\nI suppose I haven’t really noticed not being able to slow it down when I’m trying to relax. What do you mean by racing, like worrying fast, or just thinking about too many little things?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you need less sleep than you used to, or that you feel rested even if you haven’t slept much?\n\nPatient: Less sleep? Oh… I don’t know. Sleep changes so much when you get older, doesn’t it? I might wake up and think, “Well, it must be morning,” and then it’s some ridiculous hour, and then I start wondering whether I remembered to put the kettle ready—though I don’t know why I bother, it takes two seconds.\n\nBut feeling rested after hardly any sleep… I’m not sure I’ve noticed that. I wouldn’t say I suddenly need less sleep, no. It’s hard to tell what’s just age and what’s something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting irritated more easily than before, or annoyed by things that wouldn’t have bothered you in the past?\n\nPatient: Irritated more easily… I don’t know. I mean, little things can be annoying, can’t they? Like when someone leaves a trolley right in the middle of the aisle and then stands there discussing soup as if no one else exists—though I suppose I’ve always found that a bit trying.\n\nI’m not sure it’s more than before. My daughter says I get cross when the television is too loud, but honestly, some of those adverts are made to be irritating, aren’t they? So… I don’t know that I’ve noticed a real change.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

My daughter says, “Mother, just sit down,” but then I notice a crooked picture frame or some papers that need sorting, and off I go. Even at 87, I feel like I ought to be able to rest, but being still is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble relaxing, even when you’re not busy with tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not constant, exactly, but if I’m sitting through the announcements at church, or one of those senior center meetings where they read every little item—well, I get this ants-in-my-chair feeling. I’ll shift around and fuss with my papers, or I’ll decide I need coffee just so I can stand up for a minute. It’s silly, because I’m not in a hurry to be anywhere, but my body acts like I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things, even when there’s no clear reason to be?\n\nPatient: Hmm… worried? I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, at my age there are always little things to think about—appointments, whether I left the kettle on, and my neighbor’s cat keeps wandering into my garden, which is another story—but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around feeling anxious for no reason. At least, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy, even if you’re not doing much?\n\nPatient: Tired? Well… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed being unusually tired when I’m not doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities you used to like—like seeing friends, gardening, or going to church events?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, really. But not enjoying things—hmm. I don’t think I’d say that. I’d have to think about it more, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing or that you can’t slow your mind down, even when you want to relax?\n\nPatient: Racing thoughts… hmm. You start listening to every creak in the house and then wonder if the milk’s gone off.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I feel especially rested or full of energy after less sleep. If anything, I just potter about and see how the morning goes. I haven’t really noticed a change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been going to bed later or waking up earlier, and yet still felt like you had enough rest or didn’t really need much sleep at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least, I don’t think so. I do sometimes end up going to bed later, but that’s usually because I’ve got caught up in something silly, like sorting a drawer or watching the end of a programme I wasn’t even enjoying.\n\nBut feeling like I don’t need sleep—no, I wouldn’t say that. If I haven’t slept enough, I tend to know about it. I might still get up early because, well, once I’m awake I’m awake, and lying there feels pointless. But I wouldn’t say I feel especially rested from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that you didn’t really need to sleep as much as usual, like your body just didn’t require it, even if you weren’t particularly tired to begin with?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that feeling. I mean, I’ve had nights where sleep’s a bit all over the place, but not that sense of, you know, “Oh, I simply don’t need it.” If I don’t sleep, I usually notice in some way.\n\nThough sometimes I get up and start doing little things because I’m awake anyway—then I find myself in the kitchen wondering why I came in there. But needing less sleep, like my body doesn’t require it… no, I can’t say I’ve noticed that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever gone through periods where you felt you could get by just fine with very little sleep, like you didn’t really need much at all, even if you weren’t especially tired?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. No, I don’t think so. For the past year or so I’m always puttering around—fixing a drawer, arranging things, signing up to help with one more little volunteer thing. Even at 87, I feel like I ought to be able to rest, but being still is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble relaxing, even when you’re not busy with tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not constant, exactly, but if I’m sitting through the announcements at church, or one of those senior center meetings where they read every little item—well, I get this ants-in-my-chair feeling. I’ll shift around and fuss with my papers, or I’ll decide I need coffee just so I can stand up for a minute. It’s silly, because I’m not in a hurry to be anywhere, but my body acts like I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things, even when there’s no clear reason to be?\n\nPatient: Hmm… worried? I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, at my age there are always little things to think about—appointments, whether I left the kettle on, and my neighbor’s cat keeps wandering into my garden, which is another story—but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around feeling anxious for no reason. At least, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy, even if you’re not doing much?\n\nPatient: Tired? Well… I don’t know. At 87, everyone expects you to say yes, don’t they? I do sit down sometimes and think, “Goodness, that was enough for now,” but then I remember the laundry in the dryer or that I meant to call about the church flowers—last year they had the lilies delivered too early and the whole hallway smelled like a florist shop.\n\nSo, lacking energy… I’m not sure I’d say that. I haven’t really noticed being unusually tired when I’m not doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities you used to like—like seeing friends, gardening, or going to church events?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, really. I still like being around people when I go, and the garden is… well, the garden is more work than it used to be, but that may be because the weeds seem to have gotten more ambitious than I have.\n\nChurch events can be lovely, though sometimes they do go on, especially when everyone needs to make an announcement. But not enjoying things—hmm. I don’t think I’d say that. I’d have to think about it more, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing or that you can’t slow your mind down, even when you want to relax?\n\nPatient: Racing thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d call it that. Sometimes when I’m sitting down, I’ll think of one thing and then another, like whether I put the bins out or if I should write a note to Margaret—oh, she never answers her phone anyway, always says she can’t hear it ringing—but I don’t know that my mind feels like it’s racing exactly.\n\nI suppose I haven’t really noticed not being able to slow it down when I’m trying to relax. What do you mean by racing, like worrying fast, or just thinking about too many little things?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you need less sleep than you used to, or that you feel rested even if you haven’t slept much?\n\nPatient: Less sleep? Oh… I don’t know. Sleep changes so much when you get older, doesn’t it? I might wake up and think, “Well, it must be morning,” and then it’s some ridiculous hour, and then I start wondering whether I remembered to put the kettle ready—though I don’t know why I bother, it takes two seconds.\n\nBut feeling rested after hardly any sleep… I’m not sure I’ve noticed that. I wouldn’t say I suddenly need less sleep, no. It’s hard to tell what’s just age and what’s something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting irritated more easily than before, or annoyed by things that wouldn’t have bothered you in the past?\n\nPatient: Irritated more easily… I don’t know. For the past year or so I’m always puttering around—fixing a drawer, arranging things, signing up to help with one more little volunteer thing. My daughter says, “Mother, just sit down,” but then I notice a crooked picture frame or some papers that need sorting, and off I go. Even at 87, I feel like I ought to be able to rest, but being still is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble relaxing, even when you’re not busy with tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not constant, exactly, but if I’m sitting through the announcements at church, or one of those senior center meetings where they read every little item—well, I get this ants-in-my-chair feeling. I’ll shift around and fuss with my papers, or I’ll decide I need coffee just so I can stand up for a minute. It’s silly, because I’m not in a hurry to be anywhere, but my body acts like I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things, even when there’s no clear reason to be?\n\nPatient: Hmm… worried? I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, at my age there are always little things to think about—appointments, whether I left the kettle on, and my neighbor’s cat keeps wandering into my garden, which is another story—but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around feeling anxious for no reason. At least, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy, even if you’re not doing much?\n\nPatient: Tired? Well… I don’t know. At 87, everyone expects you to say yes, don’t they? I do sit down sometimes and think, “Goodness, that was enough for now,” but then I remember the laundry in the dryer or that I meant to call about the church flowers—last year they had the lilies delivered too early and the whole hallway smelled like a florist shop.\n\nSo, lacking energy… I’m not sure I’d say that. I haven’t really noticed being unusually tired when I’m not doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities you used to like—like seeing friends, gardening, or going to church events?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, really. I still like being around people when I go, and the garden is… well, the garden is more work than it used to be, but that may be because the weeds seem to have gotten more ambitious than I have.\n\nChurch events can be lovely, though sometimes they do go on, especially when everyone needs to make an announcement. But not enjoying things—hmm. I don’t think I’d say that. I’d have to think about it more, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing or that you can’t slow your mind down, even when you want to relax?\n\nPatient: Racing thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d call it that. Sometimes when I’m sitting down, I’ll think of one thing and then another, like whether I put the bins out or if I should write a note to Margaret—oh, she never answers her phone anyway, always says she can’t hear it ringing—but I don’t know that my mind feels like it’s racing exactly.\n\nI suppose I haven’t really noticed not being able to slow it down when I’m trying to relax. What do you mean by racing, like worrying fast, or just thinking about too many little things?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you need less sleep than you used to, or that you feel rested even if you haven’t slept much?\n\nPatient: Less sleep? Oh… I don’t know. Sleep changes so much when you get older, doesn’t it? I might wake up and think, “Well, it must be morning,” and then it’s some ridiculous hour, and then I start wondering whether I remembered to put the kettle ready—though I don’t know why I bother, it takes two seconds.\n\nBut feeling rested after hardly any sleep… I’m not sure I’ve noticed that. I wouldn’t say I suddenly need less sleep, no. It’s hard to tell what’s just age and what’s something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting irritated more easily than before, or annoyed by things that wouldn’t have bothered you in the past?\n\nPatient: Irritated more easily… I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track of it, to be honest. I wouldn’t say it’s been overwhelming or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel rested or energized even when you haven't slept as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. How can I help you today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is… I just can’t seem to sit still much anymore. For the past year or so I’m always puttering around—fixing a drawer, arranging things, signing up to help with one more little volunteer thing. My daughter says, “Mother, just sit down,” but then I notice a crooked picture frame or some papers that need sorting, and off I go. Even at 87, I feel like I ought to be able to rest, but being still is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble relaxing, even when you’re not busy with tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not constant, exactly, but if I’m sitting through the announcements at church, or one of those senior center meetings where they read every little item—well, I get this ants-in-my-chair feeling. I’ll shift around and fuss with my papers, or I’ll decide I need coffee just so I can stand up for a minute. It’s silly, because I’m not in a hurry to be anywhere, but my body acts like I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things, even when there’s no clear reason to be?\n\nPatient: Hmm… worried? I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, at my age there are always little things to think about—appointments, whether I left the kettle on, and my neighbor’s cat keeps wandering into my garden, which is another story—but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around feeling anxious for no reason. At least, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy, even if you’re not doing much?\n\nPatient: Tired? Well… I don’t know. At 87, everyone expects you to say yes, don’t they? I do sit down sometimes and think, “Goodness, that was enough for now,” but then I remember the laundry in the dryer or that I meant to call about the church flowers—last year they had the lilies delivered too early and the whole hallway smelled like a florist shop.\n\nSo, lacking energy… I’m not sure I’d say that. I haven’t really noticed being unusually tired when I’m not doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities you used to like—like seeing friends, gardening, or going to church events?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, really. I still like being around people when I go, and the garden is… well, the garden is more work than it used to be, but that may be because the weeds seem to have gotten more ambitious than I have.\n\nChurch events can be lovely, though sometimes they do go on, especially when everyone needs to make an announcement. But not enjoying things—hmm. I don’t think I’d say that. I’d have to think about it more, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing or that you can’t slow your mind down, even when you want to relax?\n\nPatient: Racing thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d call it that. Sometimes when I’m sitting down, I’ll think of one thing and then another, like whether I put the bins out or if I should write a note to Margaret—oh, she never answers her phone anyway, always says she can’t hear it ringing—but I don’t know that my mind feels like it’s racing exactly.\n\nI suppose I haven’t really noticed not being able to slow it down when I’m trying to relax. What do you mean by racing, like worrying fast, or just thinking about too many little things?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you need less sleep than you used to, or that you feel rested even if you haven’t slept much?\n\nPatient: Less sleep? Oh… I don’t know. Sleep changes so much when you get older, doesn’t it? I might wake up and think, “Well, it must be morning,” and then it’s some ridiculous hour, and then I start wondering whether I remembered to put the kettle ready—though I don’t know why I bother, it takes two seconds.\n\nBut feeling rested after hardly any sleep… I’m not sure I’ve noticed that. I wouldn’t say I suddenly need less sleep, no. It’s hard to tell what’s just age and what’s something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting irritated more easily than before, or annoyed by things that wouldn’t have bothered you in the past?\n\nPatient: Irritated more easily… I don’t know. I mean, little things can be annoying, can’t they? Like when someone leaves a trolley right in the middle of the aisle and then stands there discussing soup as if no one else exists—though I suppose I’ve always found that a bit trying.\n\nI’m not sure it’s more than before. My daughter says I get cross when the television is too loud, but honestly, some of those adverts are made to be irritating, aren’t they? So… I don’t know that I’ve noticed a real change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad more often than usual, even if it’s not overwhelming or constant?\n\nPatient: Down or sad… well, I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting around weeping or anything like that. Everyone has days, don’t they? Sometimes the weather’s miserable and you think, oh, what a grey old day, and then you remember the washing’s still in the machine—though I don’t know why I always start washing so late.\n\nBut more often than usual… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of it, to be honest. I wouldn’t say it’s been overwhelming or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel rested or energized even when you haven't slept as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, sometimes I sleep badly and still get up because lying there is boring, isn’t it? You start listening to every creak in the house and then wonder if the milk’s gone off.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I feel especially rested or full of energy after less sleep. If anything, I just potter about and see how the morning goes. I haven’t really noticed a change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been going to bed later or waking up earlier, and yet still felt like you had enough rest or didn’t really need much sleep at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least, I don’t think so. I do sometimes end up going to bed later, but that’s usually because I’ve got caught up in something silly, like sorting a drawer or watching the end of a programme I wasn’t even enjoying.\n\nBut feeling like I don’t need sleep—no, I wouldn’t say that. If I haven’t slept enough, I tend to know about it. I might still get up early because, well, once I’m awake I’m awake, and lying there feels pointless. But I wouldn’t say I feel especially rested from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that you didn’t really need to sleep as much as usual, like your body just didn’t require it, even if you weren’t particularly tired to begin with?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that feeling. So no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that you didn't really need to sleep as much as usual, like your body just didn't require it, even if you weren't particularly tired to begin with?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. But if I’ve not slept enough, I do usually feel it. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when your mood was unusually high or you felt unusually energetic, like you didn’t need as much rest or had more ideas than usual, even if others might not have noticed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve had anything like that, no. Even at 87, I feel like I ought to be able to rest, but being still is hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble relaxing, even when you’re not busy with tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not constant, exactly, but if I’m sitting through the announcements at church, or one of those senior center meetings where they read every little item—well, I get this ants-in-my-chair feeling. I’ll shift around and fuss with my papers, or I’ll decide I need coffee just so I can stand up for a minute. It’s silly, because I’m not in a hurry to be anywhere, but my body acts like I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things, even when there’s no clear reason to be?\n\nPatient: Hmm… worried? I haven’t really noticed being unusually tired when I’m not doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities you used to like—like seeing friends, gardening, or going to church events?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, really. I still like being around people when I go, and the garden is… well, the garden is more work than it used to be, but that may be because the weeds seem to have gotten more ambitious than I have.\n\nChurch events can be lovely, though sometimes they do go on, especially when everyone needs to make an announcement. But not enjoying things—hmm. I don’t think I’d say that. Oh… I don’t know. Sleep changes so much when you get older, doesn’t it? I might wake up and think, “Well, it must be morning,” and then it’s some ridiculous hour, and then I start wondering whether I remembered to put the kettle ready—though I don’t know why I bother, it takes two seconds.\n\nBut feeling rested after hardly any sleep… I’m not sure I’ve noticed that. I wouldn’t say I suddenly need less sleep, no. It’s hard to tell what’s just age and what’s something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting irritated more easily than before, or annoyed by things that wouldn’t have bothered you in the past?\n\nPatient: Irritated more easily… I don’t know. I still like being around people when I go, and the garden is… well, the garden is more work than it used to be, but that may be because the weeds seem to have gotten more ambitious than I have.\n\nChurch events can be lovely, though sometimes they do go on, especially when everyone needs to make an announcement. But not enjoying things—hmm. I don’t think I’d say that. I’d have to think about it more, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing or that you can’t slow your mind down, even when you want to relax?\n\nPatient: Racing thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d call it that. Sometimes when I’m sitting down, I’ll think of one thing and then another, like whether I put the bins out or if I should write a note to Margaret—oh, she never answers her phone anyway, always says she can’t hear it ringing—but I don’t know that my mind feels like it’s racing exactly.\n\nI suppose I haven’t really noticed not being able to slow it down when I’m trying to relax. What do you mean by racing, like worrying fast, or just thinking about too many little things?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you need less sleep than you used to, or that you feel rested even if you haven’t slept much?\n\nPatient: Less sleep? Oh… I don’t know. Sleep changes so much when you get older, doesn’t it? I might wake up and think, “Well, it must be morning,” and then it’s some ridiculous hour, and then I start wondering whether I remembered to put the kettle ready—though I don’t know why I bother, it takes two seconds.\n\nBut feeling rested after hardly any sleep… I’m not sure I’ve noticed that. I wouldn’t say I suddenly need less sleep, no. It’s hard to tell what’s just age and what’s something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting irritated more easily than before, or annoyed by things that wouldn’t have bothered you in the past?\n\nPatient: Irritated more easily… I don’t know. I mean, little things can be annoying, can’t they? Like when someone leaves a trolley right in the middle of the aisle and then stands there discussing soup as if no one else exists—though I suppose I’ve always found that a bit trying.\n\nI’m not sure it’s more than before. My daughter says I get cross when the television is too loud, but honestly, some of those adverts are made to be irritating, aren’t they? So… I don’t know that I’ve noticed a real change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad more often than usual, even if it’s not overwhelming or constant?\n\nPatient: Down or sad… well, I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting around weeping or anything like that. Everyone has days, don’t they? Sometimes the weather’s miserable and you think, oh, what a grey old day, and then you remember the washing’s still in the machine—though I don’t know why I always start washing so late.\n\nBut more often than usual… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of it, to be honest. I wouldn’t say it’s been overwhelming or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel rested or energized even when you haven't slept as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, sometimes I sleep badly and still get up because lying there is boring, isn’t it? You start listening to every creak in the house and then wonder if the milk’s gone off.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I feel especially rested or full of energy after less sleep. If anything, I just potter about and see how the morning goes. I haven’t really noticed a change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been going to bed later or waking up earlier, and yet still felt like you had enough rest or didn’t really need much sleep at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least, I don’t think so. I do sometimes end up going to bed later, but that’s usually because I’ve got caught up in something silly, like sorting a drawer or watching the end of a programme I wasn’t even enjoying.\n\nBut feeling like I don’t need sleep—no, I wouldn’t say that. If I haven’t slept enough, I tend to know about it. I might still get up early because, well, once I’m awake I’m awake, and lying there feels pointless. But I wouldn’t say I feel especially rested from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that you didn’t really need to sleep as much as usual, like your body just didn’t require it, even if you weren’t particularly tired to begin with?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that feeling. I mean, I’ve had nights where sleep’s a bit all over the place, but not that sense of, you know, “Oh, I simply don’t need it.” If I don’t sleep, I usually notice in some way.\n\nThough sometimes I get up and start doing little things because I’m awake anyway—then I find myself in the kitchen wondering why I came in there. But needing less sleep, like my body doesn’t require it… no, I can’t say I’ve noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever gone through periods where you felt you could get by just fine with very little sleep, like you didn’t really need much at all, even if you weren’t especially tired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve never really had a stretch where I thought, “Oh, I only need a couple of hours and I’m perfectly fine.” I might wake early and then start fussing about with something, you know, putting a cup away or checking the curtains—funny how curtains never hang quite right—but that’s more because I’m awake, not because I feel I don’t need sleep.\n\nIf I’ve had very little sleep, I usually feel it one way or another. So no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that you didn't really need to sleep as much as usual, like your body just didn't require it, even if you weren't particularly tired to begin with?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I can have a funny night here and there, but not that feeling that my body just doesn’t need sleep.\n\nSometimes I’ll be awake and think, well, I may as well get up, and then I’ll start pottering about—though half the time I forget what I was doing halfway through. But if I’ve not slept enough, I do usually feel it. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when your mood was unusually high or you felt unusually energetic, like you didn’t need as much rest or had more ideas than usual, even if others might not have noticed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve had anything like that, no. I mean, I have days where I’m a bit more lively, especially if there’s something to sort out—though then I end up starting three little jobs and forgetting the first one. Like yesterday I meant to tidy one drawer and somehow found an old birthday card, and then I was thinking about who sent it…\n\nBut unusually high mood, or not needing rest, or lots more ideas than usual—no, I can’t really say I’ve noticed a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you take less pleasure in the things you used to enjoy, like gardening, seeing friends, or helping out at church, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, no. I still do things, and some days I enjoy them well enough—though with gardening I’ll go out to do one thing and end up fussing over a completely different pot, then forget the first job. My neighbor had some lovely geraniums last year, actually, bright red ones…\n\nSorry, what was it—pleasure in things? I don’t think I’d say I’ve lost that, really. I can’t point to anything definite like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like yesterday I meant to tidy one drawer and somehow found an old birthday card, and then I was thinking about who sent it…\n\nBut unusually high mood, or not needing rest, or lots more ideas than usual—no, I can’t really say I’ve noticed a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you take less pleasure in the things you used to enjoy, like gardening, seeing friends, or helping out at church, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, no. My neighbor had some lovely geraniums last year, actually, bright red ones…\n\nSorry, what was it—pleasure in things? I don’t think I’d say I’ve lost that, really. I can’t point to anything definite like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t really get as much enjoyment out of things as you used to, even if you still do them—like maybe you go to the garden but it doesn’t feel as satisfying, or you help out at church but it doesn’t lift your mood the way it used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any clear way. It’s not constant, exactly, but if I’m sitting through the announcements at church, or one of those senior center meetings where they read every little item—well, I get this ants-in-my-chair feeling. I’ll shift around and fuss with my papers, or I’ll decide I need coffee just so I can stand up for a minute. It’s silly, because I’m not in a hurry to be anywhere, but my body acts like I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things, even when there’s no clear reason to be?\n\nPatient: Hmm… worried? I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I mean, at my age there are always little things to think about—appointments, whether I left the kettle on, and my neighbor’s cat keeps wandering into my garden, which is another story—but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around feeling anxious for no reason. At least, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy, even if you’re not doing much?\n\nPatient: Tired? Well… I don’t know. At 87, everyone expects you to say yes, don’t they? I do sit down sometimes and think, “Goodness, that was enough for now,” but then I remember the laundry in the dryer or that I meant to call about the church flowers—last year they had the lilies delivered too early and the whole hallway smelled like a florist shop.\n\nSo, lacking energy… I’m not sure I’d say that. I haven’t really noticed being unusually tired when I’m not doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities you used to like—like seeing friends, gardening, or going to church events?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, really. I still like being around people when I go, and the garden is… well, the garden is more work than it used to be, but that may be because the weeds seem to have gotten more ambitious than I have.\n\nChurch events can be lovely, though sometimes they do go on, especially when everyone needs to make an announcement. But not enjoying things—hmm. I don’t think I’d say that. I’d have to think about it more, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing or that you can’t slow your mind down, even when you want to relax?\n\nPatient: Racing thoughts… hmm. I’m not sure I’d call it that. Sometimes when I’m sitting down, I’ll think of one thing and then another, like whether I put the bins out or if I should write a note to Margaret—oh, she never answers her phone anyway, always says she can’t hear it ringing—but I don’t know that my mind feels like it’s racing exactly.\n\nI suppose I haven’t really noticed not being able to slow it down when I’m trying to relax. What do you mean by racing, like worrying fast, or just thinking about too many little things?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you need less sleep than you used to, or that you feel rested even if you haven’t slept much?\n\nPatient: Less sleep? Oh… I don’t know. Sleep changes so much when you get older, doesn’t it? I might wake up and think, “Well, it must be morning,” and then it’s some ridiculous hour, and then I start wondering whether I remembered to put the kettle ready—though I don’t know why I bother, it takes two seconds.\n\nBut feeling rested after hardly any sleep… I’m not sure I’ve noticed that. I wouldn’t say I suddenly need less sleep, no. It’s hard to tell what’s just age and what’s something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting irritated more easily than before, or annoyed by things that wouldn’t have bothered you in the past?\n\nPatient: Irritated more easily… I don’t know. I mean, little things can be annoying, can’t they? Like when someone leaves a trolley right in the middle of the aisle and then stands there discussing soup as if no one else exists—though I suppose I’ve always found that a bit trying.\n\nI’m not sure it’s more than before. My daughter says I get cross when the television is too loud, but honestly, some of those adverts are made to be irritating, aren’t they? So… I don’t know that I’ve noticed a real change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or sad more often than usual, even if it’s not overwhelming or constant?\n\nPatient: Down or sad… well, I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting around weeping or anything like that. Everyone has days, don’t they? Sometimes the weather’s miserable and you think, oh, what a grey old day, and then you remember the washing’s still in the machine—though I don’t know why I always start washing so late.\n\nBut more often than usual… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track of it, to be honest. I wouldn’t say it’s been overwhelming or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel rested or energized even when you haven't slept as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, sometimes I sleep badly and still get up because lying there is boring, isn’t it? You start listening to every creak in the house and then wonder if the milk’s gone off.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I feel especially rested or full of energy after less sleep. If anything, I just potter about and see how the morning goes. I haven’t really noticed a change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been going to bed later or waking up earlier, and yet still felt like you had enough rest or didn’t really need much sleep at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least, I don’t think so. I do sometimes end up going to bed later, but that’s usually because I’ve got caught up in something silly, like sorting a drawer or watching the end of a programme I wasn’t even enjoying.\n\nBut feeling like I don’t need sleep—no, I wouldn’t say that. If I haven’t slept enough, I tend to know about it. I might still get up early because, well, once I’m awake I’m awake, and lying there feels pointless. But I wouldn’t say I feel especially rested from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that you didn’t really need to sleep as much as usual, like your body just didn’t require it, even if you weren’t particularly tired to begin with?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that feeling. I mean, I’ve had nights where sleep’s a bit all over the place, but not that sense of, you know, “Oh, I simply don’t need it.” If I don’t sleep, I usually notice in some way.\n\nThough sometimes I get up and start doing little things because I’m awake anyway—then I find myself in the kitchen wondering why I came in there. But needing less sleep, like my body doesn’t require it… no, I can’t say I’ve noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever gone through periods where you felt you could get by just fine with very little sleep, like you didn’t really need much at all, even if you weren’t especially tired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve never really had a stretch where I thought, “Oh, I only need a couple of hours and I’m perfectly fine.” I might wake early and then start fussing about with something, you know, putting a cup away or checking the curtains—funny how curtains never hang quite right—but that’s more because I’m awake, not because I feel I don’t need sleep.\n\nIf I’ve had very little sleep, I usually feel it one way or another. So no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that you didn't really need to sleep as much as usual, like your body just didn't require it, even if you weren't particularly tired to begin with?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I can have a funny night here and there, but not that feeling that my body just doesn’t need sleep.\n\nSometimes I’ll be awake and think, well, I may as well get up, and then I’ll start pottering about—though half the time I forget what I was doing halfway through. But if I’ve not slept enough, I do usually feel it. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when your mood was unusually high or you felt unusually energetic, like you didn’t need as much rest or had more ideas than usual, even if others might not have noticed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve had anything like that, no. I mean, I have days where I’m a bit more lively, especially if there’s something to sort out—though then I end up starting three little jobs and forgetting the first one. Like yesterday I meant to tidy one drawer and somehow found an old birthday card, and then I was thinking about who sent it…\n\nBut unusually high mood, or not needing rest, or lots more ideas than usual—no, I can’t really say I’ve noticed a period like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you take less pleasure in the things you used to enjoy, like gardening, seeing friends, or helping out at church, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, no. I still do things, and some days I enjoy them well enough—though with gardening I’ll go out to do one thing and end up fussing over a completely different pot, then forget the first job. My neighbor had some lovely geraniums last year, actually, bright red ones…\n\nSorry, what was it—pleasure in things? I don’t think I’d say I’ve lost that, really. I can’t point to anything definite like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t really get as much enjoyment out of things as you used to, even if you still do them—like maybe you go to the garden but it doesn’t feel as satisfying, or you help out at church but it doesn’t lift your mood the way it used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any clear way. I still like going out to the garden, though I might go out meaning to water one thing and then notice a weed and then—well, before I know it I’m doing something else entirely.\n\nAnd church, I still feel glad to help when I’m there. Sometimes I’m tired after, but I think that’s just being 87, isn’t it? I wouldn’t say it doesn’t lift me the way it used to. I’ve not really noticed a loss of enjoyment like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have to push yourself more to get interested in things you used to enjoy, even if you still do them—like maybe you go to the garden, but you don’t really feel the same satisfaction you used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I have to push myself, not really. I mean, sometimes I’ll stand there in the garden and think, now what did I come out here for? And then I’ll see the bird feeder needs filling, or a leaf has gone yellow, and I’m off doing that instead.\n\nBut the satisfaction… no, I can’t say I’ve noticed it’s gone. I still like it once I’m doing it. I just get a bit sidetracked, that’s all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
